- VO: "...ever since," (23.91-24.84), then the take's own comma pause (24.84-25.00, continuing at 0.00-0.05).
- Sound: landing thunk at 24.06, under the VO. A soft lid clunk at 24.375, 8 dB under the VO; the reversed slurp stops with it. The chase resolves on 24.375 into the caper theme's pickup, leading into bar 1. From 24.45 there is only surf, swash and the pickup: the same sound as frame 0.

### 3. Hook

- 0.0 s VO: "You can't walk to Lānaʻi." (5 words, about 1.6 s). The turn, "Unless you put some ocean in the freezer.", starts at 1.86 s, and the sea starts pouring into the freezer on "ocean" (2.95 s).
- FRAME 0 (and the last frame) shows one absurd thing that reads at phone size: a paper tourist with a suitcase at a Maui waterline, holding open a white chest freezer that stands in the surf, frost curling out. Lānaʻi lies right across the water, labelled.
- It does not read as a travel Short: there is a freezer in the sea; the LĀNAʻI label is the series' plain paper name slip; bar 1 is a sneaky pizzicato caper theme, not a ukulele.
- The anomaly escalates inside the swipe window: 1.0 s the compressor kicks on (THUNK, frost puff); 1.2 s the backwash runs too far and a rope of seawater climbs into the freezer (slurp); 1.86 s "Unless"; 2.40 s the lid flies open; 2.95 s the arc becomes a torrent and the sea starts falling.
- A freezer pulling water uphill, plus the slurp, makes the drain read as cartoon magic, never as a tsunami drawback.
- Probe (ytsearch, 2026-11-08): "walk to lanai": best Short 33K, off-topic. "maui lanai molokai one island ice age": best 9.3K, off-topic. "maui lanai land bridge": no on-topic Short. "ocean in the freezer" and "freeze the ocean sea level drop": only frozen-sea novelty Shorts and global sea-level maps, no land-bridge premise. The ferry crowd exists: "Why Hawaii's Islands Aren't Connected by Boats" has 5.4M views as a Short.
- No greeting, no channel name, no number anywhere in the Short.

### 4. Payoff

The payoff is a reframe, then a twist, then the button.
1. **Gag and spectacle (4.95-13.03).** "Lānaʻi snaps onto Maui. Molokaʻi snaps onto Lānaʻi." A paper slip lands on each island as it is named, and a click sounds as each gap closes. The rim traces the merged land, with Kahoʻolawe left out. "Sorry, ferry." He hops over the stranded ferry, which lets out a sad toot. "You're grounded." He wags his finger at it.
2. **Reframe (13.33-15.26).** "The Ice Age did this for real." The IT HAPPENED stamp lands over the land bridge, with Lānaʻi's flank showing its modern shoreline as a bathtub ring high above. The silly premise turns out to be history: the cartoon freezer was the ice sheets.
3. **Twist (15.61-20.47).** "The catch? Nobody ever walked it. People hadn't made it here yet." The NOBODY WALKED IT slip lands, the music stops dead, the tourist stares into the camera, then looks down at his aloha shirt: he is the anachronism. A real "huh", it heads off the certain "there were no humans then" comment, and it is funny.
4. **Button and loop (20.47-25.0).** The sea creeps back: time-lapse cloud shadows, a reversed meltwater slurp, gentle foam, comic chase music; no rumble, no surge. He scrambles home along the real last link, which goes under right behind his heels. The LĀNAʻI slip flutters back out of the freezer and he props the lid open again. "Then the ice melted, the sea crept back, and ever since," runs into "you can't walk to Lānaʻi. Unless you put some ocean in the freezer." On replay the hook means something new: the only freezer that ever worked was the Ice Age.

The Short never spends the long-form's payoff (no 56,000 ft, no dent, no Everest) and reuses none of the long-form's lines.

### 5. Loop check

**Audio.** Last line into first line: "Then the ice melted, the sea crept back, and ever since, you can't walk to Lānaʻi. Unless you put some ocean in the freezer."
- Plain English: a "since" clause completed by a main clause. On a first view, "You can't walk to Lānaʻi." still stands alone.
- On replay it closes a joke circle: the ice melted, so you would need a freezer to do it again.
- Both halves come from ONE TTS take with the text rotated. The cut sits inside the take's own comma pause after "since," (Rodney's comma pauses measure 0.08-0.15 s); at most 0.1 s of room tone is added (about 0.16 s at the end, 0.05 s at the start). No time-stretching; prosody, breath and room tone continue across the seam.
- Music: 96 BPM, 10 bars = 25.000 s. Bar 10's chase resolves on 24.375 into the caper pickup, which lands on bar 1's downbeat at frame 0. The surf bed is a loop. The refill audio (reversed slurp) ends at 24.375 when the lid settles; from 24.45 the bed is surf, swash and the pickup, identical to frame 0. No rumble, no roar, no fade.
- Export: exactly 750 frames and exactly 25.000 s of audio; AAC encoder priming trimmed and checked with ffprobe; played looped for 3 cycles on a phone; plus one cold start from 0.00 to confirm VO1 sounds like an opening.

**Picture.** Frames 684-749 (22.80-24.967 s) and frames 0-151 (0-5.05 s) are ONE continuous render of Shot A, render time t = -2.2 to 5.05. Frame 749 is t = -0.033 and frame 0 is t = 0: consecutive frames of the same render, so the seam is not a cut at all.
- Everything matches by construction: the camera on the frame-0 push path at the same speed since 22.80; the tourist's pose (leaning in 12°, right hand on the lid, suitcase in his left hand, front foot in the swash); the freezer lid at 25° with the frame-0 frost curl and the compressor off; the swash touching his foot and the freezer's base at the seam; the ferry's position and bob phase; the LĀNAʻI and MAUI slips at identical positions; cloud shadows at normal speed since 24.60; same sun and grade.
- The only cut near the end is the visible whip-pan at 22.55-22.80, 2.2 s before the seam. Everything that differs from frame 0 finishes by 24.80, with at least 0.15 s between events; from 24.85 to 25.00 only the swash runs up.
- Captions switch hard by design (none from 24.84; YOU CAN'T at 0.05), so the seam metrics are measured on the picture layer before captions.
- Gates: frame-749-to-frame-0 SSIM no lower than the shot's median consecutive-frame SSIM; first/last MAE ≤ 5/255; the self-concatenated temporal_scan shows no "cut" or "flash".

### 6. Facts

Spine = brief section 4. Ledger = research/ep02/script/ledger_part1.json. GMRT = s02_grid/mauinui.asc (about 60 m cells). Checks: s02v3_scripts/bottleneck.py, raceline.py, maximin.py, pools.py (output bott.npy).

1. **"You can't walk to Lānaʻi." TRUE.** Lānaʻi is a separate island; the shallowest crossing to West Maui (the ʻAuʻau Channel) is about 50-53 m down. Ledger C12-03 gives about -50 m near 20.885N 156.758W; maximin.py finds the best route's lowest point at -53.4 m. The State Data Book's 77 m is the maximum depth along a fixed line, not the saddle.
2. **"Unless you put some ocean in the freezer."** A joke premise, and an honest cartoon of the real mechanism: in an ice age, water leaves the ocean and is stored as ice on land, so the sea falls (C12-04, NASA Sea Level Change). The line says "put ocean in the freezer" (take water out of the sea and store it frozen), not "freeze the ocean": sea ice floats and would not lower the sea. The freezer stands on the beach, never floats.
3. **"Lānaʻi snaps onto Maui." TRUE.** Spine #5 and C12-03: the Lānaʻi-West Maui saddle is about -50 m. bottleneck.py: Lānaʻi stays joined to the Launiupoko beach up to a sea level of -52 m (2 m steps). Shot B closes the gap as the level passes -53 m, on "snaps".
4. **"Molokaʻi snaps onto Lānaʻi." TRUE.** C12-03: the Molokaʻi-Lānaʻi saddle is about -78 m on the Kalohi side (panel measured -78 to -82 m). Molokaʻi joins through Lānaʻi, not directly: the direct Pailolo saddle is about -238 to -242 m, so the Pailolo stays a turquoise inlet in every drained shot (USGS Volcano Watch 2003: East Molokaʻi and West Maui "remained connected via Lānaʻi"). Penguin Bank joins Molokaʻi at about -60 m and sits inside the rim (spine #5). Kahoʻolawe stays separate down to -159 m (C12-03), so it is dimmed and left outside the rim (C12-06). VERIFY (a): re-run sill.py on the HMRG 50 m grid before lock (margins 37 m or more; no change expected).
5. **"Sorry, ferry. You're grounded."** A joke. The paper ferry "runs aground" on the dry floor. A real Maui-Lānaʻi passenger ferry runs; it is never named and the paper ferry carries no livery.
6. **"The Ice Age did this for real." + IT HAPPENED. TRUE.** In the last Ice Age the sea stood about 120 m (400 ft) lower because water was locked up in ice sheets (C12-04, NASA Sea Level Change). Maui, Lānaʻi and Molokaʻi were connected, most recently about 18,000 years ago (C12-05, USGS Volcano Watch 2003); Grigg et al. 2002 describe limestone bridges between them at the last glacial maximum. Our drain stops at -122 m, inside the 120-130 m range of estimates. The mechanism is spoken two lines later ("Then the ice melted"). The stamp lands on the land and Lānaʻi, and the twist corrects the implication about the walker 1 s later.
7. **"The catch? Nobody ever walked it. People hadn't made it here yet." + NOBODY WALKED IT. TRUE. NEW LEDGER LINE; VERIFY (f) to close before lock.** First settlement of Hawaiʻi: AD 940-1130 at 95% HPD, most probably AD 1000-1100 (Athens, Rieth & Dye 2014, American Antiquity 79(1):144-155); other models put it later, in the 13th century (Wilmshurst et al. 2011, PNAS). Spine #9 (Nihoa, about AD 1000) agrees; ledger C4-07 warns the Nihoa dates conflict, so no date is spoken. The bridge was dry only during glacial low stands; the sea has stood near today's level for thousands of years and the shallowest crossing is about 50 m down, so it was underwater long before anyone arrived. "Nobody" means no person; nothing is said about animals.
8. **"Then the ice melted, the sea crept back, and ever since, ..." TRUE.** Spine #5 and C12-04. USGS 2003: "Molokaʻi became isolated, and then the land bridging Lānaʻi and Maui was submerged." Grigg et al. 2002: about 120 m of post-glacial rise flooded the bridges. "Crept" is honest: the rise took thousands of years, and the picture marks the refill as a time-lapse. The refill order follows the data: the Molokaʻi-Lānaʻi link (-78 m) floods first, the ʻAuʻau crest (-53 m) last; the heel-lick happens on that crest.
9. **Picture-only details** (from the grid, not invention).
   - Race geography (bottleneck.py, raceline.py, maximin.py): the straight 265° line from Launiupoko to Lānaʻi is dry at -122 m (lowest point -78 to -80 m, 7-8 km out). On the refill, the last dry link is the crest at 20.885N 156.758W (-53.4 m). The middle leg home stays dry until the crest floods, but its best route hugs the south Lahaina shore (about 20.867N 156.674-156.684W), so it is never shown; the whip-pan at 22.55 is the ellipsis. The final approach is only the Launiupoko shelf (-33 m at 1 km out, -20 m at 0.5 km); every hop lands on ground at least 1 m above the water.
   - Lahaina town (20.873N 156.677W) bearings: 323° from the frame-0 camera, 333° from Shot C, 99° from the crest (Shots D, E, G1): at least 30° outside every frame edge.
   - Pools (pools.py): at -122 m the -136 m hollow at 20.761N 156.809W is OPEN SEA, an inlet. The real enclosed pools are at 20.786N 156.708W (0.32 km², floor -128 m), 20.774N 156.756W (0.26 km², floor -124 m) and a 3.3 km² basin at 20.771N 156.687W (floor -148 m). Turquoise pools only there; the tourist's route (20.83-20.89N) stays north of all of them.
   - Sounds: no gull; the cricket is a sound gag, not a claim.
10. **Big numbers: ZERO** on screen and spoken. 400 ft, "under 200 ft", 18,000 years, 150,000-200,000 years and the settlement date live only in the description and pinned comment.

Spelling: Molokaʻi and ʻAuʻau correct throughout; the Grigg paper is cited by its exact printed title ("Au'au Channel").

**Still open (VERIFY before lock):** (a) saddles and crest on the HMRG 50 m grid; (b) rights for the tourist photo (the long-form cut-out), the ferry photo and the freezer photo, or our own photo or illustration; (c) lexicon check on wehewehe.org and a full phone-speaker listen; (d) Barlow ʻokina: drawn with the U+2018 glyph substitution (Anton already passes, short03/okina_test.png), and Ā confirmed in latin-ext; (e) Launiupoko outside the August 2023 burn area on the county map; (f) add the settlement line to the ledger.

### 7. Renders: new for this Short vs reused

**Reused as-is** (same data, assets and settings; only seen through new vertical cameras):
- The long-form Ch1 0:12.9-0:18 Maui Nui drain: terrain tile and bake (HMRG/GMRT blended into ETOPO 2022), water-plane shader and foam, the -122 m rim polygon (#FFD23F), the Penguin Bank lobe, the Kahoʻolawe dim and desaturate mask, seabed materials.
- The long-form 0:18-0:23 golden-hour land-bridge pass: flight data, sun and grade (re-flown for 9:16 as Shot D).
- The long-form 0:23.6 paper tourist: the same public-domain cut-out, matte, base tab, contact and directional shadows, S11 hop physics.
- Type and paper system: Barlow Condensed 800, Anton, paper slips, the Juice Check stamp, the POST grade.
- SFX library: riser, paper flap, hop whoosh, thunk, impact_deep, stamp cue, whoosh, puzzle click.

**New, for this Short only**
1. **SHOT A, the loop's spine.** ONE continuous shore-level render from t = -2.2 to 5.05 (7.25 s), placed at 22.80-25.00 and 0-5.05. A close coastal tile from NCEI/USGS 3DEP coastal lidar (1-3 m) blended into the Maui Nui tile; beach, wet-basalt and reef-flat materials; the shared near-surface water pass and periodic loop-clock water (shorts_plan.md 5B); tilt-shift over the foreground; a cloud-shadow layer easing from 40x to 1x over t = -2.2 to -0.4; water-level curve: the refill tail (-36 to 0 m), then 0 m, a slow leak into the freezer from 1.2 to 2.95, and a fast fall to about -30 m by 5.05; the incoming half of the whip (t = -2.2 to -1.95) rendered inside it. Motion-test it FIRST (U5). FALLBACK, decided before anything else is built: a 200 m low oblique over the same beach (heading 258.5°, pitch -12°, same push); the tourist becomes an S11 standee at 35-40% of frame height, the freezer goes to cartoon scale, all events and times stay the same.
2. **The FREEZER PROP.** A paper cut-out of a real white chest freezer (our own photo, or a verified public-domain one) with a separate lid card on a hinge. Rig: propped at 25°, flung open (bang on the hinge stop), swung back to 25°; cabinet shudder; lid light; a frost-curl sprite on the loop clock; frosting-over (3.55); meltwater dribble (23.60). Water-rope VFX: a twisting stream peeling off the swash and pouring into the freezer (1.20-5.05), growing into a torrent on "freezer"; reversed and gentle at 23.60.
3. **Water-level curves**, in one file. Drain, keyed to words: -53 m on "snaps" #1, -78 m on "snaps" #2, -122 m at 9.30. Refill: -122 m at 20.75, -110 at 21.28, -85 at 21.85, -53.4 at 22.30 (the crest), -36 at 22.80, -14 at 23.50 (the ferry floats), -3.5 at 24.06, -1.5 at 24.375 (the lid settles), 0 at 24.85.
4. **SHOT B:** a regional vertical camera from the w345 previz, about 1° lower and about 10% tighter; a hidden cut inside the lift blur at 5.05. The material pass must replace the previz's grey-tan shelf placeholder with slate-olive carbonate and dark basalt.
5. **Camera moves:** the lift (4.55-5.05), the crash zoom (9.85-10.07), two whip-pans (13.05-13.32 and 22.55-22.80).
6. **SHOT C:** a ground camera on the dry shelf 400 m off Launiupoko, 3 m up, heading 275°.
7. **SHOT D:** the re-flown pass on the crest approach (heading 275°), with a rise and tilt to show Lānaʻi's 0 m "bathtub ring".
8. **SHOT E:** a front-on medium shot on the crest, heading 5°.
9. **SHOT G1:** a side-tracking race shot along the crest, heading 5°; two gentle foam fronts meet at 20.885N 156.758W at 22.30.
10. **Rigs.** The tourist as a split-pin puppet: an arm for the lid (hold, fling, swing back) and the finger wag; a turning head; the suitcase as a separate piece; a 0.15 s Paper-Mario flip; a mid-skip skid. The paper ferry: a public-domain pre-1929 photo of a small passenger steamer with no livery, or our own paper illustration; bob, ground, flop and right-itself animations; a sad toot and a happy toot.
11. **UI:** paper slips LĀNAʻI (with the small Myth Juice stamp), MAUI, MOLOKAʻI and NOBODY / WALKED IT; the IT HAPPENED stamp; the house caption track.
12. **Audio:** one rotated TTS take (3 candidates); a 96 BPM, 10-bar music cue built to loop (caper theme A, bouncy theme B, a comic full-band stop, tiptoe plucks, a double-time chase resolving into theme A's pickup); a loopable surf bed; compressor THUNK and hum, lid bang, the water-rope slurp, meltwater dribble and reversed slurp, lid clunk, cricket, the two ferry toots.

**Cut from the draft** (de-cloning and panel fixes): the -400 FEET odometer and the race clock; the LAST ICE AGE kicker and its scrim; the ONE ISLAND headline and the MAUI NUI slip; the 5 s crane-up that repeated the merge; the rumble, the roar and the music cut to rumble; the gull; the 6° lean; the bath plug and chain (integrator: the plug belongs to Short 1 only).

**QC gates:** phone sheet at 390 px; every cap ≥ 77 px (the smallest here is 90); all text x ≤ 849; luma ≥ 70 on every shot; gate.py tan ≤ 10% on every drained shot; place-match diff of 0 (Lānaʻi at 0.81, 4.95 and 8.40; Maui at 6.15; Molokaʻi at 6.99); Lahaina bearing gate (the town at least 10° outside every frame edge, no 2023 burn imagery); frame 749 vs frame 0 continuity; one 3-cycle looped playback on a phone.

The long-form does not change. Every Short render is a separate pass from shared data and assets; no long-form shot, line, cue, timing or file is edited.

### 8. Title, description, link

**Release:** D+1. **Related video:** the long-form "Why Can't You See Most of Hawaii? (Look Down)", so its link shows on the Shorts player.

**Title:** You Can't Walk From Maui to Lānaʻi (Unless...) (46 characters). Passes the niche regex (no drained/drain, hidden/hiding, bottom/beneath, nobody, found; no Everest) and carries no "plug" or "freezer". Backup: "Has Anyone Ever Walked From Maui to Lānaʻi?" (43; the twist answers it).

**Description:**

> In the last Ice Age, so much seawater was locked up in ice that the ocean sat about 400 feet lower. Maui, Lānaʻi and Molokaʻi were joined into one island, part of what scientists call Maui Nui (most recently about 18,000 years ago). Lānaʻi would touch Maui if the sea dropped less than 200 feet. Kahoʻolawe stayed separate: its land link to Maui probably broke 150,000 to 200,000 years ago. And nobody ever made the walk. People reached Hawaiʻi only about a thousand years ago, long after the sea came back.
>
> The full film drains the ocean around Hawaiʻi all the way down, to the true base of the mountain:
> Why Can't You See Most of Hawaii? (Look Down)
>
> Every depth in this Short comes from real seafloor survey data. The freezer is a joke; the ice sheets were real.
> Bathymetry: GMRT (Global Multi-Resolution Topography, Lamont-Doherty Earth Observatory); University of Hawaiʻi SOEST HMRG multibeam synthesis; NOAA ETOPO 2022.
> Sources: USGS Hawaiian Volcano Observatory, Volcano Watch, "Once a big island, Maui County now four small islands" (2003); NASA Sea Level Change (Ice Age sea level); Grigg, R.W. et al. (2002), "Drowned reefs and antecedent karst topography, Au'au Channel, S.E. Hawaiian Islands", Coral Reefs 21: 73-82; Athens, J.S., Rieth, T.M. and Dye, T.S. (2014), "A Paleoenvironmental and Archaeological Model-Based Age Estimate for the Colonization of Hawai'i", American Antiquity 79: 144-155.
> Photos: [public-domain credits for the tourist, ferry and freezer photos, filled in after the rights check].

**Tags:** hawaii, maui, lanai, molokai, maui nui, ice age, sea level, ice age sea level, land bridge, hawaii without water, drained ocean, hawaiian islands, geography. The drain words go in the tags and description only, never in the title.

**Pinned comment** (no emojis, live within 5 minutes, owner's OK): "Nobody ever made this walk: Polynesian voyagers reached Hawaiʻi roughly a thousand years ago, long after the sea had covered the land bridge. Have you ever taken the ferry across to Lānaʻi?"

**Captions:** burned-in house captions, plus our own uploaded caption file with the native spellings (Lānaʻi, Molokaʻi) from the lexicon caption_map. Never leave YouTube's auto-captions, which would write "Lanai".

### 9. Panel must-fixes and how this version answers them

Panel score on the draft: 66. All must-fixes are in: narration rewritten new (no long-form line reused); the anomaly starts by 1.2 s and "Unless" lands by 1.86 s; the refill is a creeping time-lapse tide with no rumble or roar; the "nobody walked it" twist and the pinned settlement line; one slip per island as each is named, and the slow crane repeat cut; built to the real take with the seam in the take's own comma pause; the G2 hidden cut replaced by one continuous Shot A render; fact #8 corrected (the -136 m hollow is an inlet); Lahaina kept out of frame; typos fixed; a Shot A fallback chosen in advance; the -400 FEET odometer and the scrim kicker removed.

### Integrator changes (2026-11-08)

1. Captions follow the house spec (shorts_plan.md section 4): Anton cap 80, centred x 460 / y 1515. The writer's cap-77 box at y 1452-1529 and its flag to the owner are replaced by the one house position for all 7.

Here the idiom is new narration inside a new sentence, it is the loop device, and the physical gag is new (the hat lifts and comes back, the missing side re-forms, he ducks). ### 1. Narration and TTS

**Voice:** "Rodney - Clear Educator" (`eY2VJs4Gi8QKG2RV01sS`), `eleven_v4`, passed explicitly. Same settings for every take: the locked ep02 long-form settings, speed 1.0; never above 1.05. Generate only after the owner OKs this text, then re-time every cue from the real word timestamps.

**On-screen order** (61 words, a loop of about 26.4 s; estimate from Rodney's measured eleven_v4 take, 32 words in 12.16 s; model 0.19 s per syllable + 0.36 s per comma + 0.45 s sentence-final lengthening):
> This volcano is missing a whole side. Long before anyone lived here, it slid into the sea. One of the largest landslides on Earth. Drain the ocean, and there it is. In pieces. The biggest piece? Bigger than Manhattan. And the wreckage runs for more than a hundred miles. So if you're ever up here, hold on to your hat, because...

**Exact TTS strings** (three takes, identical settings):
- **TAKE A** (the story, 2.76-17.91 s): "Long before anyone lived here, it slid into the sea. One of the largest landslides on Earth. Drain the ocean, and there it is. In pieces. The biggest piece? Bigger than Manhattan."
- **TAKE C** (the key-statistic line in its own take, 18.66-21.54 s): "And the wreckage runs for more than a hundred miles."
- **TAKE B** (the loop seam, ONE performance): "So if you're ever up here, hold on to your hat, because... this volcano is missing a whole side."
  - Split at the zero crossing inside the pause after "because...". The tail plays at 21.84-25.85 s; the head ("this volcano is missing a whole side.") plays at 0.05-2.51 s.
  - The head is the main clause, so it carries a falling statement cadence and no hedge word up front.

**Takes:** render each take 3 times (`"take": 1, 2, 3` in the tts.py segment) with identical settings; the owner picks by ear. ASR check with faster-whisper small.en and medium.en: accept only if "landslides", "Manhattan" and "a hundred miles" come back as scripted.

**TAKE B acceptance:** the head played alone must sound like a confident cold open (full onset energy on "This", a firm fall on "side", no trailing continuation tone). If none of 6 takes passes, use a separate take of "This volcano is missing a whole side." as the head and keep the seam pause at 0.6 s or more; the owner judges that seam by ear.

**Edit pauses** (set from word timestamps, never typed into the TTS): 0.25 s after "side."; 0.30 after "sea."; 0.55 after "Earth."; 0.66 in total between "ocean," and "and" (the water visibly falls first); 0.75 after "is."; 0.45 after "pieces."; 0.55 after "piece?" (the guess beat, covered by the camera's dive); 0.75 after "Manhattan." (the slam lands); 0.30 after "miles."; 0.55 after "because..." plus 0.05 s head silence = 0.60 s across the cut (pad with room tone if TAKE B's natural pause is shorter).

**Flex rule:** if L comes out under 25.5 s, add up to 0.5 s after "Manhattan." and up to 0.3 s after "pieces."; if over 29 s, trim pauses first. Never cut the hook or the loop line; never speed Rodney past 1.05.

**Pronunciation:** Rodney speaks no Hawaiian word in this Short ("here" replaced the draft's spoken "Oʻahu"), so no respelling and no U+02BB go into the TTS text. Lexicon notes for every Hawaiian word in the package (on screen, title, description, tags, pinned comment, production notes):
- Oʻahu: oh-AH-hoo [oˈʔahu]; TTS token if ever voiced "oh-AH-hoo"; never "oh-WAH-hoo".
- Hawaiʻi: hah-WHY-ee [həˈwɐiʔi], light catch before the final "ee".
- Koʻolau: koh-oh-LAO [koˈʔolɐu], "lau" as in cow.
- Nuʻuanu: noo-oo-AH-noo [nuʔuˈwɐnu], four syllables with a glottal break.
- Pali: PAH-lee [ˈpɐli]; never PAL-ee or PAY-lee.
- Kailua: kye-LOO-ah [kɐiˈluwɐ]. Kāneʻohe: kah-neh-OH-heh [kaːneˈʔohe]. Mōkapu: moh-KAH-poo [moːˈkɐpu]. Lanihuli: lah-nee-HOO-lee [lɐniˈhuli]. These four are not yet in lexicon.json; add them.
- Lexicon status is DRAFT (not test-rendered, not checked on wehewehe.org, not heard by a Hawaiian speaker). This Short voices none of them.

### 3. Hook

FRAME 0 is a high oblique over Oʻahu's windward Koʻolau, already pushing in. Beyond the island's straight northeast coast, a translucent white lobe is attached: the volcano's missing side (crisp white edge, faint contours, the real coast and sea visible through it). It fills about a third of the frame and ends in a clean footprint line on the sea. Above it, in open sea, the headline OʻAHU’S / MISSING SIDE (Anton, cap 96/120, upper third, with the correct 6-shaped ʻokina). Trade-wind clouds stream over the crest and a light sweep runs along the ghost's edge.

VO at 0.05 s: "This volcano is missing a whole side." (7 words; "missing" at about 1.05-1.44 s; the line ends at about 2.5 s, from the measured Rodney rate). The first caption card, THIS VOLCANO, is on screen from frame 0.

How this answers the swipe panel:
1. No longer a travel snapshot: the odd thing, a piece of a familiar island outlined as missing, is the subject of frame 0 and reads at 390 px (m_f0_phone390.jpg).
2. The line points at something visible: "This volcano" over the Koʻolau range, "a whole side" over the glowing lobe.
3. Works with the sound off: the headline names the anomaly and the place; burned captions carry every word.
4. No hedge word: "About" is gone, and the hook is the main clause of TAKE B, with an acceptance test and a fallback.
5. Realistic timing, from Rodney's one measured take.

Why the frame is no longer the eye-level lookout: from the lookout (bearings.py, 110 m hero grid) Kāneʻohe Bay is at 348°, the Lanihuli-side crest at 315°, Mōkapu at 29° and Kailua at 55°; an honest mirrored ghost (break line through the Kailua-area caldera, USGS) stands only 3-5° above the horizon, 5-8 km away, and renders as a flat pale band that reads as haze (pair2.jpg). From the high oblique the same ghost is a large, unmistakable missing piece (reg_f.png).

The premise is open: probes on 2026-11-08 ("where did this volcano's other side go", "oahu missing side volcano landslide", "nuuanu landslide oahu") found no Short on it; the best on-topic Short is the 1K "Hawaii Landslide: HUGE!". Hōlei Sea Arch collapse Shorts (1.6M and 371K) show "Hawaii falls into the sea" is live in the feed. No "taller than Everest", no number before 18 s, no greeting.

### 4. Payoff

The payoff escalates in four steps; the top step comes last, and each image over-delivers the words.
1. **"Drain the ocean, and there it is. In pieces." (9.5-14.0 s).** On the same fixed camera where the pieces slid under, the sea drains; the faint ghost pieces turn into the real dark basalt blocks as they break the surface, exactly where they sank. Cause and effect in one uncut shot.
2. **"The biggest piece?"** (a guess beat over the dive, base contour traced in white), then **"Bigger than Manhattan."** A true-size paper Manhattan from a real Landsat photo, Central Park visible, drops from the top edge and slams flat onto the block along its long axis with room to spare: dust, a shake, a paper wobble. The picture shows about 6-7 times Manhattan's area while the line modestly says "bigger". The laugh beat.
3. **"And the wreckage runs for more than a hundred miles."** (the peak) The camera pulls back to the slide framing; the debris footprint draws on from Oʻahu's own coast to the toe, dwarfing the island it came from. MORE THAN / 100 / MILES rolls in and lands on "hundred" and "miles": the one number, a quarter-plus of the frame tall.
4. **The gag payoff,** starting 0.3 s after "miles" (no dead air after the climax). The sea refills and the camera pushes back to the opening frame. The recurring paper tourist pops up on the Pali ("So if you're ever up here"); on "hold on to your hat" a trade-wind gust flips his hat up, and on "because..." it drops back onto his head. Then the missing side re-forms right behind him; he takes one look and ducks behind the crest. The idiom lands literally, the wind behaves as it really does at the Pali (the hat goes up and comes back; nothing falls off the cliff), and the unfinished sentence completes itself in the opening line.

Everything after the last fact (21.54-26.4 s) is narrated, except the 0.6 s seam pause.

### 5. Loop check

**Last line into first line:** "So if you're ever up here, hold on to your hat, because... this volcano is missing a whole side."
- Natural English: "hold on to your hat, because..." is the standard brace-yourself idiom; literal here (the gust has just flipped his hat; the missing side has just re-formed behind him).
- Recorded as one performance (TAKE B) and split in the pause after "because", so the intonation across the cut is continuous. The pause is 0.60 s (0.55 tail + 0.05 head); the duck's paper flap and the new gust sit inside it.
- Fallback (owner's ear): a separate head take with the pause kept at 0.6 s or more.

**Last frame against first frame.** Frame L-1 and frame 0 come from one deterministic shot function of t mod L, so the step from L-1 to 0 is one motion step, not a match cut.
- Camera: the same C0 push-in line at 1.2%/s, zero roll; the return push from R3 merges into this line with matched velocity by 23.5 s, almost 3 s before the seam.
- Ghost: full fill and edge in both frames (finished re-forming at 25.95).
- Tourist and hat: hidden behind the crest in both frames since 25.95.
- Headline OʻAHU’S / MISSING SIDE at full opacity and the same position in both (in from 26.35).
- Clouds and the edge light sweep repeat with period L. Grade and sun identical.
- Captions: none in the last frame (the BECAUSE... card ended at 26.20); THIS VOLCANO appears at frame 0. Caption cards switch hard by design, so the seam metrics are measured on the picture layer before captions.
- The big camera moves and the gag end before the final 0.45 s.

**Audio.** The VO ends on "because..." with a 5-8 ms fade. The new gust whoosh starts at 26.2 and runs across the cut. The wind bed and music are rendered wrapped with a 0.5 s tail-into-head crossfade. Music: N bars with N x 4 x 60 / bpm = L (13 bars at 118.2 bpm, or 12 at 109.1, or 14 at 127.3; pick after the real TTS). Bar N has no cadence and resolves into bar 1.

**QC gate:** first-to-last-frame MAE ≤ 5/255 and SSIM ≥ 0.97 on the picture layer; concatenate the Short with itself and run temporal_scan.py (no "cut" or "flash" at the join); the owner plays 3 consecutive loops on a phone with sound. Ear verdicts are his.

### 6. Facts

"Spine" = brief.md section 4; "ledger" = research/ep02/script/ledger_part1.json (production fact check of 2026-11-08).
1. **"This volcano is missing a whole side."** (on screen OʻAHU’S MISSING SIDE). Spine #6 (the Nuʻuanu slide, the Koʻolau collapse). Worded to the ledger's primary-source version (C12-21b): USGS Volcano Watch, "Oahu, Niihau and Kauai": "The caldera was bisected by the catastrophic collapse of the Nu'uanu landslide"; USGS 2016 geologic tour of Oʻahu: Koʻolau "forms the east side of the island". Ledger wording: "Its whole northeast side is lying out here." "Half" was dropped from hook and title: C12-21a marks it disputed (secondary sources only). VERIFIED.
2. **"Long before anyone lived here, it slid into the sea."** Spine #6, "more than a million years ago" (ODP Leg 200: 1.8-2.4 Ma; Wikipedia 1-1.5 Ma) against settlement around AD 1000. True under every estimate; no age spoken. VERIFIED.
3. **"One of the largest landslides on Earth."** Spine #6; USGS 2016: "one of the largest landslides in the world"; Moore et al. 1989: Hawaiian debris avalanches "rank among the largest on Earth". Never "the largest". VERIFIED.
4. **"Drain the ocean, and there it is. In pieces."** Spine #6; USGS 1995: the slide "deposited numerous blocks on the sea floor as far as 100 miles northeast of the island". Phrased as a thought experiment. VERIFIED.
5. **"The biggest piece? Bigger than Manhattan."** Spine #6 (USGS: "the largest of these blocks is about the same size as Manhattan Island"); brief section 7: if the block measures larger, say "bigger than Manhattan". Ledger C12-23b/23a: on GMRT the Tuscaloosa block's summit is -2,691 m and its base at the -4,100 m contour about 27 x 18 km (~380 km²); Moore et al. about 30 x 17 km; USGS 2016 "over 29 km (18 mi) long"; Manhattan about 59 km², 21.6 x 3.7 km. VERIFIED. The long-form says "bigger than Manhattan" too. Production check: lay the true-size TIGER outline on the grid's base contour before the slam (long axis 27 vs 21.6 km).
6. **"And the wreckage runs for more than a hundred miles."** (MORE THAN / 100 / MILES). Spine #6 (about 200 km); USGS 2016 "about 200 km (120 mi)"; ODP "more than 200 km"; USGS 1995 blocks "as far as 100 miles". Ledger C12-22: "more than a hundred miles" is the only wording all sources support. Matches the long-form. VERIFIED.
7. **"So if you're ever up here, hold on to your hat"** An idiom and a gag. Hawaiʻi DLNR (ledger C12-18): "Winds are usually so strong that one can lean against the wall of wind." No wind speed given.

**Visual honesty**
- Ghost: a sketch, never labelled; the description calls it a sketch. It is the surviving leeward flank mirrored across a NW-SE line through the caldera (USGS 2016: "in the Kailua area"), with a crest-height plateau out to the mirrored crest distance, then the mirrored leeward profile down to sea level about 16 km out; VE 1.0 (short03v3/ghost3.py).
- Six pieces: schematic; their resting places are the real block positions, the biggest at Tuscaloosa (about 22.07 N, 157.08 W).
- No tsunami imagery and no wave rings (brief must-fix 3).
- Hat physics follow C12-18: the trade wind blows from the NE, up the windward cliff, so the hat goes up and comes back. The draft's hat over the parapet is cut (it reversed the real wind). C12-18 also notes the 1795 Battle of Nuʻuanu: nothing falling off the Pali is ever the joke. The tourist ducks down the leeward side at map scale.
- Manhattan: true size, US Census TIGER outline, public-domain Landsat 9 imagery, on the real block.
- Footprint: VERIFY that the USGS I-2809 outline layer is legible enough to trace; otherwise use the Moore et al. 1989 figure.
- Depths: HMRG multibeam, GMRT and ETOPO 2022; the first crown uses GMRT's -2,691 m (Wikipedia gives -2,756 m).
- No Everest, no 56,000 ft, no dent. One spoken number ("a hundred") and one on-screen number (100).

Everything on screen is a new vertical render at 1080x1920, 30 fps. Nothing is cropped from the long-form, and the long-form is not changed.

**New cameras, assets and animation**

Drain keyframes as in the table. Seabed material pass: dark basalt, dark olive and grey-brown sediment drape at value ≤ 0.40, wet sheen, foam rim, turquoise moat.

Music from engine/tools/music.py, so the plan's licence check covers it too.

### 8. Title, description, link

**Release:** D+3. At upload the owner sets the Short's Related Video to "Why Can't You See Most of Hawaii? (Look Down)". Links in Shorts comments are not clickable. Nothing is uploaded, scheduled or set by us.

**Title:** Where Did This Volcano's Other Side Go? (39 characters; a concrete question; none of drain/drained, hidden/hiding, bottom/beneath, nobody, found; no Everest; passes the niche regex; exact-title probe 2026-11-08: 0 on-topic matches; does not repeat the frame-0 headline, which names the place). Alternative for a later test: "Where Did Oʻahu's Other Side Go?" (32).

**Description (paste as is):**

> More than a million years ago, the Koʻolau volcano, which built the east side of Oʻahu, split through its summit, and its whole northeast side slid into the Pacific. It is called the Nuʻuanu landslide, one of the largest landslides on Earth. Drain the ocean off the island's northeast coast and the pieces are still there. The biggest block, Tuscaloosa Seamount, is about 30 km (19 mi) long and 17 km (11 mi) wide: bigger than Manhattan. The debris runs more than 100 miles (about 200 km) across the sea floor.
>
> The full story of what's under Hawaiʻi: Why Can't You See Most of Hawaii? (Look Down)
>
> Every depth comes from real seafloor maps. The pale shape of the missing side is a sketch, not a measurement: the surviving west flank of the volcano, mirrored.
> Seafloor: University of Hawaiʻi SOEST HMRG multibeam synthesis; GMRT; NOAA ETOPO 2022. Island terrain: USGS 3DEP. Manhattan: Landsat 9 imagery (USGS/NASA) and U.S. Census TIGER outline.
> Sources: USGS Volcano Watch, "Oahu, Niihau and Kauai"; USGS Volcano Watch, "A geologic tour of the Hawaiian Islands: Oʻahu"; Moore et al. 1989, "Prodigious submarine landslides on the Hawaiian Ridge" (USGS); Moore, Normark and Holcomb 1994, "Giant Hawaiian landslides"; ODP Leg 200 Initial Reports, Chapter 1 (Nuuanu Landslide); USGS Geologic Investigations Series I-2809, "Hawaii's Volcanoes Revealed" (Eakins et al. 2003).
>
> #Oahu #Hawaii #Geology

Notes: all readable text spells Oʻahu, Koʻolau, Nuʻuanu and Hawaiʻi with U+02BB; the long-form title is quoted exactly as launched; hashtags and tags use ASCII search spellings on purpose.

**Tags:** Nuʻuanu landslide, Nuuanu slide, Oahu, Oʻahu, Pali Lookout, Koolau volcano, Tuscaloosa Seamount, Hawaii without water, ocean drained Hawaii, largest landslide on Earth, Hawaii geology. The drain words live in the tags, the description and the spoken "Drain the ocean" at about 9.5 s; the title and on-screen text carry none.

**Pinned comment** (live within 5 minutes, posted by the owner): "Have you stood at the Pali Lookout? Did your hat make it?" Heart and reply to visitor stories; reply to any correction within 48 hours.

**How it feeds the long-form:** it reuses the film's Ch2 world (the Nuʻuanu drain, the Manhattan block at true size, the debris footprint, the recurring paper tourist and his hat at the Pali), plays as a complete looped story with its own payoff, spends nothing from Ch3-Ch6 (no Everest, no 56,000 ft, no dent), and matches the long-form's fact wording ("bigger than Manhattan", "more than a hundred miles", "whole side").

**Fact-check sources:** USGS Volcano Watch: Oahu, Niihau and Kauai (https://www.usgs.gov/news/volcano-watch-oahu-niihau-and-kauai); USGS Volcano Watch: A geologic tour of the Hawaiian Islands, Oʻahu (https://www.usgs.gov/center-news/volcano-watch-a-geologic-tour-hawaiian-islands-o-ahu; re-fetched 2026-11-08: "Koʻolau's caldera is located in the Kailua area"; "about 200 km (120 mi)"; a block "over 29 km (18 mi) long"); ODP Leg 200 IR ch.1 (https://www-odp.tamu.edu/publications/200_IR/chap_01/c1_3.htm); USGS: Prodigious submarine landslides on the Hawaiian Ridge (https://www.usgs.gov/publications/prodigious-submarine-landslides-hawaiian-ridge); USGS: Giant Hawaiian landslides (https://www.usgs.gov/publications/giant-hawaiian-landslides); USGS I-2809 (https://pubs.usgs.gov/publication/i2809); Hawaiʻi DLNR: Nuʻuanu Pali State Wayside (https://dlnr.hawaii.gov/dsp/parks/oahu/nuuanu-pali-state-wayside/); Wikipedia: Tuscaloosa Seamount (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuscaloosa_Seamount).

|---|---|

### Integrator changes (2026-11-08)

1. Captions follow the house spec (shorts_plan.md section 4): Anton cap 80, centred x 460 / y 1515 (was x 464; the note about Short 6 sitting 25 px higher is resolved, since all 7 now share one position).

### 1. Narration and TTS

**As heard (one loop):**
> Hawaiʻi's Big Island is headed here. A hot spot builds a volcano. The sea floor carries it away. Slowly, it sinks. Rain carves it into canyons. Then a sliver of rock that people once farmed. Then one last tooth. Then just a ring of reef. Under it, a volcano about twenty-eight million years old, sunk out of sight. Every island on this line takes the same trip. So,

**TTS take** (ONE take of 74 words; generate it 3 times (`"take": 1, 2, 3`) and pick by ASR (small.en and medium.en) and the owner's listen; 68 of the 74 words go on air):
> Hawaii's Big Island is headed here. A hot spot builds a volcano. The sea floor carries it away. Slowly, it sinks. Rain carves it into canyons. Then a sliver of rock that people once farmed. Then one last tooth. Then just a ring of reef. Under it, a volcano about twenty-eight million years old, sunk out of sight. Every island on this line takes the same trip. So, Hawaii's Big Island is headed here.

- Voice "Rodney - Clear Educator" (`eY2VJs4Gi8QKG2RV01sS`), `eleven_v4`, passed explicitly (tts.py still defaults to Brian); the locked long-form settings.
- The TTS text uses plain "Hawaii's" (lexicon.json); captions map it to "Hawaiʻi's" through caption_map.
- The take's CLOSING sentence, "So, Hawaii's Big Island is headed here.", holds the loop seam. The on-air VO1 is the part of that sentence after "So,". The take's opening read of the same sentence is a spare that gives VO2 a natural run-in.
- Split the take into sentence clips at its natural gaps, place each clip at the times below and fill gaps with the take's own room tone. Never split inside "same trip. So, Hawaii's...".
- T is the cut, in the middle of the comma gap between "So," and "Hawaii's". Planned T = 29.77 s (frame 893).

**Pronunciation** (lexicon.json; still DRAFT: close its open items 1-3 before the narration render)
- Hawaiʻi's (the only Hawaiian word spoken; in the take twice, once on air per play): TTS text plain "Hawaii's", no U+02BB unless a test render shows the voice handles it. Target /həˈwaɪ.ʔi/ + 's: three syllables, light glottal catch before the last "ee". If the test render gives two syllables, use the lexicon alt "hah-WHY-ee's" (keep w, not v). Never accept the two-syllable "huh-WHY-eez". caption_map turns "Hawaii's" and "hah-WHY-ee's" into "Hawaiʻi's".
- On screen, in the description or in this sheet only (never spoken here): Maui "Maui", alt "MAO-ee" (never "MOW"); Kauaʻi "kah-WAH-ee", alt "kow-WAH-ee" (never "KOW-eye"); Nihoa "nee-HOH-ah"; Kīlauea "kee-lao-WAY-ah", alt "kee-lah-oo-AY-ah" (avoid "KILL-uh-WAY-uh"); Mauna Kea "MAO-nah KAY-ah"; Mauna Loa "MAO-nah LOH-ah" ("Loa" two syllables); La Pérouse (French) "lah peh-ROOZ"; Lalo "LAH-loh"; Papahānaumokuākea "pah-pah-HAH-now-moh-koo-ah-KEH-ah" (the monument's own guide: "Pa-pa-hah-now-mo-koo-ah-keh-ah"); Pūhāhonu (Gardner Pinnacles; pin only, rule-derived) "poo-hah-HOH-noo".
- Not in lexicon.json; add before any use beyond this sheet (rule-derived; check on wehewehe.org): Waimea "wai-MAY-ah"; Nā Pali "nah PAH-lee".

The Big Island slides down out of frame, keeping its orange fill; the under-glow stays behind with it. From here the islands get only the white rim (they are off the heat). The trail fades out between y 1300 and 1420, so it never reaches the caption band.

The ring is about 350 px wide, centred at (488, 1000); the trail arrives at the ring from below. On "reef" (19.40-19.90) the white rim redraws. The card glides into its frame-0 pose (centre (300, 356), -4°), settles at 29.35 with a tiny paper bounce, then bobs on the loop clock. The headline fades in 29.10-29.40 (opacity 0 to 1 with a 12 px upward settle), identical to frame 0.

- VO: a pause, then "So," 29.26-29.62 (no caption).

**29.40-29.77 (T) · PURE ORBIT on P, in exactly frame 0's state**
- Picture: in frame the ring, its rim, the deep-blue water, the card and the headline; no label, caption, trail, rings, pins, number or glow. CUT at T to frame 0.
- Text: "HAWAIʻI" card + "HEADED HERE", as at frame 0.
- VO: the comma gap after "So,"; VO1 restarts at 0.05 after the cut.
- Sound: ambience and music sample-continuous across the cut.

**Escalation check.** The largest gap between visual events is about 2.1 s. Events (s): 0.0 moving frame; 2.05 card pop; 2.25 whip; 2.52 Big Island; 2.85 glow; 4.15 pulse; 5.85 trail; 6.05 hop; 7.45 Maui; 8.40 settle; 8.85 hop; 10.25 Kauaʻi; 11.40 hop; 12.70 Nihoa; 13.80 terraces; 14.95 hop; 16.15 pop-up; 16.55 wobble; 17.55 hop; 19.40 rim; 20.20 glass sea; 22.12 "28"; 23.95 number out; 24.25 sea clouds; 25.10 pull-out; 26.45 sweep; 26.95 card rises; 27.45 ride; 28.25 dive; 29.10 headline.

**Mix.** -14 LUFS integrated (±1.5 LU), true peak ≤ -1.0 dBTP; VO at least 12 LU over the music; ambience at -26 to -28 dB; SFX 10-12 dB under the voice. No SFX motif more than 3 times (paper flap 3; hop whoosh A 2, B 1, C 1, D 1). No sting at the end.

### 3. Hook

- VO1 at 0.05 s: "Hawaiʻi's Big Island is headed here." Six words, the place name first; "Big Island" by 1.12 s; "here" lands on the reef ring at 1.7-2.2 s. It uses the ledger's direction framing, not certainty (C5-19).
- FRAME 0 (already moving, readable with the sound off in under 1 s) shows the start state and the end state together: in the upper third the series postcard (a tilted paper card with a public-domain USGS HVO photo of a Kīlauea lava fountain, printed "HAWAIʻI"); directly under it "HEADED HERE" (Anton cap 104, white with dark stroke, no box, no accent word); below, the real Midway reef ring in true colour, orbiting at 2°/s, 77% of the frame width, with a white rim. The read: lava island, then "headed here", then a thin ring of reef in empty ocean. The question: how does a lava-fountain island turn into that?
- Risk and mitigation: the packaging lab found postcards on thumbnails read as travel. Here the card shows lava, not a beach, covers only 37% of the frame width over a real-Earth image, and leaves at 2.05 s on a whip.
- Fallback hook (also from C5-19): "This is the road Hawaiʻi's Big Island is on." The loop line then becomes "So, this is the road Hawaiʻi's Big Island is on." Integrator note: this echoes the long-form's s146 ("That's the road the Big Island is on."), so use it only if the owner asks for the softer framing.

### 4. Payoff

Three steps over the last 10 s, each answering the hook's "here"; every stage is a real island in its real position; nothing morphs.
1. **Glass sea and the one number (20.2-24.9).** On "Under it" the sea turns clear: a whole dark-basalt volcano (real GEBCO shape, VE 1.0) spreads out under the little ring of reef. "ABOUT 28 MILLION YEARS" lands on "twenty-eight" while the volcano is fully visible and the reef rim is dimmed, so the number dates the volcano, not the coral (C5-01). On "sunk out of sight" the water clouds back and the volcano disappears.
2. **The line was the trip (26.45-27.05).** The pull-out shows that the yellow trail, laid behind each hop since 5.85 s, is one line up the real Hawaiian Ridge; on "this line" a glow sweeps up it.
3. **The postcard makes the trip (27.45-29.40).** On "takes the same trip" the Big Island postcard rises out of the hot-spot glow, slides up the line and dives with the camera onto Midway, landing exactly where it was at frame 0. The Big Island and the ring are finally together on screen, and the opening "here" turns from a claim into a conclusion: "Every island on this line takes the same trip. So, Hawaiʻi's Big Island is headed here."

### 5. Loop check

**Last line into first line:** "...Every island on this line takes the same trip. So, Hawaiʻi's Big Island is headed here. A hot spot builds a volcano. The sea floor carries it away..." A premise, then its conclusion; on first play the opening still stands alone.

**Audio.** Both sides of the seam come from one Rodney take ending "...same trip. So, Hawaii's Big Island is headed here." The cut sits in the middle of the take's comma gap between "So," and "Hawaii's", with a 6 ms equal-power crossfade on room tone, so pitch, pace and room tone match. T planned at 29.77 s (frame 893); conform to the real word times. The ocean-swell ambience and the music bed are both seamless loops of exactly T (music 14 bars at 3360/T, about 112.9 BPM), no fade, no cadence. The only other VO join (the take's end spliced to its start) is hidden in the whip gap at 2.19-2.69 s, on room tone.

**Picture (same camera, same state).**
- Camera: the last frame is t = T - 1/30 on the opening path P(t - T): target 28.208N 177.373W, range 24.8 km, pitch -55°, vertical FOV 50°, same look offset; heading 299.93° against 300.00° at frame 0 (one frame of orbit).
- State: same sun; glass effect off; reef rim at 100%; the postcard at its frame-0 pose on the same loop-clock bob; "HEADED HERE" at identical pixels; no label, caption, trail, pin, number or under-glow.
- Settling: the dive reaches P by 29.40, the card settles at 29.35, the headline by 29.40: 0.37 s of pure orbit before the cut. "So," is spoken over the settled frame-0 state, with no caption.
- Animated layers: every time-driven layer runs on a loop clock τ = t mod T with periodic functions at k/T (waves, foam, glint shimmer, caustics, the card bob k = 4, any cloud drift).
- Visual rhyme: the card that leaves Midway at 2.05 s rides the chain back and lands in the same spot on "So, ...headed here".

**QC gate:** first/last-frame MAE ≤ 5/255 and SSIM ≥ 0.98 at 1080x1920; concatenate the Short with itself and run temporal_scan.py (no "cut" or "flash"; optical flow across the cut equals the flow from frame 0 to frame 1); frame 1 not duplicated at the end; no audio click or level step in a 400 ms window around the cut; a person watches 10 consecutive loops on a phone with sound on.

### 6. Facts

Each claim with its spine line (#), its ledger ID (research/ep02/script/ledger_part1-3.json), or VERIFY.
1. **VO1, loop line, headline, title** ("Hawaiʻi's Big Island is headed here" / HEADED HERE / "Where Hawaiʻi's Big Island Is Headed"): ledger C5-19 (verified with wording): "That's where the Big Island is headed", direction not certainty, because whether an island becomes an atoll depends on reef growth against sinking. Builds on spine #8, #11, #12, #13. The long-form now says "That's the road the Big Island is on" (s146).
2. **"A hot spot builds a volcano."** Spine #8; C4-01. The generic "a volcano" keeps every later "it" honest over the differently labelled islands (C3-13). The under-glow is a picture of the hot spot (C4-01 allows a glowing plume as a picture); never "a plume from the core" or "it never moved".
3. **"The sea floor carries it away."** Spine #8; C4-01 (the Pacific plate carries each volcano west-northwest). No rate spoken.
4. **"Slowly, it sinks."** (over Maui) Spine #4; C12-09b ("these islands sink as they age"). No rate spoken (if ever needed, C3-06: "nearly a tenth of an inch a year", 2.4 mm at Hilo). The Maui Nui shelf is not spotlighted (C12-07: it drowned mainly because the sea rose after the Ice Age). Oʻahu is not a stage (C12-09a: rising on the flexural bulge).
5. **"Rain carves it into canyons."** (Kauaʻi) C4-05: shield volcanism ended about 4 million years ago, after which the canyons and cliffs began to form.
6. **"Then a sliver of rock that people once farmed."** Spine #9; C4-06 ("sliver of rock" is fair; never the smallest or "barely above water"); C4-07 (dryland-agriculture terraces; no date, no head count). Terrace lines only from the published site map (VERIFY; with no map, no lines).
7. **"Then one last tooth."** Spine #10; C4-08 (USFWS: "the last remnant of the original volcano"). The label keeps it local to French Frigate Shoals; the whole-chain view pins Gardner Pinnacles (Pūhāhonu), where basalt still shows (C4-09). The wobble is an obvious paper gag.
8. **"Then just a ring of reef."** Spine #11-12; C5-02 ("reef" or "reef rock", never pure "coral"). Why the reef forms a ring is not claimed (C5-07).
9. **"Under it, a volcano about twenty-eight million years old, sunk out of sight."** / ABOUT 28 MILLION YEARS. Spine #11; C5-01 (the age belongs to the volcano; the reef cap is younger; "ABOUT" stays; never 28.7 or the outdated 17.9 Ma); C5-03 (drills hit basalt about 516 ft under Sand Island); C5-02 (never "steadily"). The glass-sea volcano is the real GEBCO 2024 shape at VE 1.0; substrate colours are interpretive.
10. **"Every island on this line takes the same trip."** Spine #8; C3-13; C4-01.
11. **Islands and trail:** every island a real shape at its real position (HMRG + ETOPO for the main islands, GEBCO 2024 for the northwest chain, Landsat drapes), VE 1.0, logged in the per-shot data manifest; the trail follows the real ridge crest (GEBCO 2024).
12. **Kīlauea's summit glow:** C3-12 (episodic summit eruption; episode 56 began 5 Nov 2026). VERIFY at render time; otherwise only the diagram under-glow.
13. **Photos and position:** the postcard photo (USGS HVO, Kīlauea lava fountain) and the La Pérouse photo (NOAA or USFWS) must be public domain (VERIFY rights and credit); VERIFY La Pérouse at 23.769N 166.261W against the USFWS or NOAA chart.
14. **Spellings:** HAWAIʻI, KAUAʻI, LA PÉROUSE, Lalo, Papahānaumokuākea and Pūhāhonu per C4-14 and lexicon.json.
15. **Description claims:** "riding the Pacific plate northwest" (spine #8, C4-01); "each island shows a later stage of the same trip" (C3-13); "a ring of reef on a volcano about 28 million years old" (C5-01, C5-02); Lalo (C4-08); "Nihoa can be visited only with a permit" (C4-07); the reef-against-sinking caveat (C5-19, C5-17b).
16. **Pinned comment:** Nihoa permit-only (C4-07); Midway closed to tourists: VERIFY on the USFWS Midway Atoll NWR page on publish day.

Not used: Everest, 56,000 ft, the dent, "taller than", "bigger than you think", "future", any island ages beyond Midway's. One big number shown and spoken.

### 7. Renders: new for this Short vs reused

Rendered natively at 1080x1920, 30 fps; no long-form frame is cropped and the long-form is not changed.
1. **Midway opening and closing orbit P(t):** target 28.208N 177.373W, range 24.8 km, pitch -55°, vertical FOV 50°, heading 300° + 2°/s x t, ring centre at (488, 1080); Landsat 8/9 15 m pan-sharpened drape (Sentinel-2 10 m if cleaner) on GEBCO 2024; 6 px white reef rim; deep water at mean luma ≥ 70; a periodic loop clock for waves, foam, glint, caustics.
2. **Postcard asset and animation:** card 400 x 270, #F6F1E4 with a 3 px #DED2B8 edge, plain stamp, soft contact shadow; public-domain USGS HVO lava-fountain photo; print "HAWAIʻI" in Anton cap 80; motion: loop-clock bob, hinge pop at 2.05 s, flight to Kīlauea (print fades before any shrink), rise from the glow at 26.95 s, ride up the line, settle into the frame-0 pose.
3. **Whip:** 8 frames with sub-frame motion blur (N 6-8); the world whips straight down the screen while the card stays in screen space.
4. **Big Island vertical oblique:** heading 300°, pitch -50°, island at 85% of frame width; 55% orange fill, white rim; a new "hot spot" under-glow (an emissive radial light under the southeast flank, seen through the water); the real summit glow only if Kīlauea is erupting at render time.
5. **Trail line:** new data product, a ridge-crest polyline from the Big Island to Midway traced on GEBCO 2024 (a small script that follows the crest between island centroids); drawn per hop (10 px yellow core, under-stroke 8 px wider, glow, head dot); fades out between y 1300 and 1420; glow sweep in the whole-chain view.
6. **One continuous eased flight spline:** Big Island to Maui (157 km), Kauaʻi (360 km), Nihoa (272 km), French Frigate Shoals (450 km), Midway (1,215 km); hops of 1.4 / 1.4 / 1.3 / 1.25 / 2.0 s, cubic ease-in-out, apex about 0.35 x the hop distance, heading 300° throughout; Kauaʻi framed for raking light on Waimea.
7. **La Pérouse standee:** a new cut-out of a public-domain NOAA or USFWS photo (cutout.py + card.py: warm 3 px paper edge, colour spill removed, base tab); standee() on its true coordinates at 38% of frame height, hinge pop from 35°; loose-tooth wobble ±6°, two decaying cycles over 1.0 s.
8. **Midway glass sea:** pose W (range 60 km, pitch -38°, easing to 80 km and -30°); a new water-shader mode (about 1-2 h of work: absorption x 0.05, 20% turquoise tint, glint and caustics kept, depth haze); seabed by substrate (dark basalt, grey-olive sediment, cream reef top; no tan); reef-rim dimming to 40% during the number.
9. **Whole-chain pull-out and dive:** CineGlobe STORY preset at about 5,000 km altitude, heading 298°; white rings on the stops and a Gardner Pinnacles pin (r ≥ 11 px), no overlaps; log-altitude curves easing out onto P.
10. **UI:** the HEADED HERE headline; island labels in Barlow 800 cap 84, top-left; the number block (ABOUT cap 80, 28 in Anton cap 300, MILLION YEARS cap 84); the house caption track. Safe-area mock with the real font files: short04v2/layout.py and v2_layout_sheet.jpg (all text within x 80-849 and y ≥ 206; every cap ≥ 80 px).
11. **Audio:** one Rodney take (3 generations); ambience and music beds loop-cut to exactly T; new SFX: the sink tone, the glassy muffled swell, the paper slide, the wobble "boing".

**Reused as-is from the long-form build:** terrain and data bakes (HMRG + ETOPO for the main islands; GEBCO 2024 for the northwest chain; the Midway, French Frigate Shoals and Nihoa patches; the Landsat drapes); the per-island terrain of the TIME MACHINE flight; the Nihoa terrace overlay geometry (only if from the published site map); the La Pérouse location; the Midway ring patch and the reef-cap-on-volcano data; the chain pull-back framing; the Big Island massif bake; the type system; the SFX library (whip, impact_deep, paper flap, swell, hop whooshes, rain hiss, surf boom); the music library, Rodney's voice and the lexicon. No longer needed from the draft: the Ch4 plume and plate cut-away, the age-bar gauge, the gleam sprite.

### 8. Title, description, link

**Title:** Where Hawaiʻi's Big Island Is Headed (36 characters; the place name in the first 3 words; the ledger's direction framing (C5-19); no number; passes the clone-word regex; keeps the ʻokina per the 2026-11-08 spelling rule, with plain "Hawaii" in hashtag and tags). If the owner prefers the long-form's plain spelling: "Where Hawaii's Big Island Is Headed" (35). Backup: "Every Hawaiian Island Takes the Same Trip" (41). Probe 2026-11-08 (short04v2/probe.txt): "where hawaii's big island is headed", "big island will become an atoll" and "every hawaiian island same journey" return no on-premise Short; near misses "How Does a Volcanic Island Become a Coral Atoll?" (1.5K) and "Will Hawaii Get a New Island?" (1.5K); the nearest proven premise is "How Do Atolls Form?" (613K); the spent neighbours are "Hawaii Is Much Bigger Than You Think" (14M) and "How Long Is Hawaii, Really?" (7.4M), and our whole-chain view stays at 1.8 s.

**Description (paste as is):**

> Hawaiʻi's Big Island is riding the Pacific plate northwest, away from the hot spot that is building it. Follow it up the island chain and each island shows a later stage of the same trip: Maui, Kauaʻi, Nihoa, La Pérouse Pinnacle at French Frigate Shoals (Lalo in Hawaiian), and finally Midway, a ring of reef on a volcano about 28 million years old.
>
> That is the direction the Big Island is headed. Whether a sinking island keeps a reef on top depends on how fast the reef grows, so this shows the road, not a promise of how it ends.
>
> Want to see what lies under the Big Island itself? The full film looks down: Why Can't You See Most of Hawaii? (Look Down)
>
> Every island here is real and in its real place, and heights are not exaggerated. The sea is shown clear for a moment so you can see the volcano under Midway's reef. The glow under the Big Island marks the hot spot; it is a picture, not lava. The postcard and the paper cut-out of La Pérouse Pinnacle are not to scale. Nihoa, French Frigate Shoals and Midway are part of Papahānaumokuākea Marine National Monument; Nihoa can be visited only with a permit.
>
> Sources:
> - USGS Hawaiian Volcano Observatory, Evolution of Hawaiian Volcanoes
> - USGS Volcano Watch, Island of Hawaiʻi sits at end of a long, old chain of 125 volcanoes
> - USGS Volcano Watch, A geologic tour of the Hawaiian Islands: Kauaʻi and Niʻihau

> - U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, Midway Atoll National Wildlife Refuge

> Data and images:

>
> #Hawaii #Geology #Volcano

**Link to the long-form:** in YouTube Studio the owner sets the Short's Related video to "Why Can't You See Most of Hawaii? (Look Down)"; the description names the film. This is the "Midway reef cap on a sunken volcano" funnel slot (niche report 5.2.7). The clear-water look under Midway previews the film's "look down" premise without spending the 56,000 ft or the dent. Nothing is uploaded, scheduled or posted without his OK.

**Pinned comment:** "Kauaʻi's canyons, Nihoa's cliffs or Midway's reef: two of the three are almost impossible to visit. Which would you pick anyway?" (Nihoa permit-only, C4-07; VERIFY Midway's visitor status on the USFWS refuge page on publish day.)

### 9. Panel must-fixes and how this version answers them

Panel score on the draft: 66. All 8 must-fixes are in: the certainty framing replaced everywhere (C5-19: "headed", no "future", no "in 28 Million Years"); frame 0 shows the start state and HAWAIʻI (the lava postcard over the ring, landing back in the final 0.4 s); "ring of reef", never "coral"; the 28 visibly the volcano's age (clear water through "old", reef rim dimmed); the hot-spot block cut to 3.3 s, in-world, northwest up throughout; pronunciation aligned with the lexicon and LA PÉROUSE with the É; a burned-in caption track; description and pinned comment fixed and the Maui Nui shelf not spotlighted.

### 1. Narration and TTS

**Voice:** "Rodney - Clear Educator" (`eY2VJs4Gi8QKG2RV01sS`), model `eleven_v4`, the long-form's settings (longform/ep02-hawaii/script/tts_spec.json, default voice settings). Pass the voice explicitly (tts.py still defaults to Brian). TTS text uses plain ASCII "Hawaii" (lexicon entry "hawaii"); screen text keeps the ʻokina. No bracket or break tags; the number spelled as spoken.

**Segments file** (tts.py, per-segment takes; previous/next text sent automatically). Planned start, then the pause after the segment:

| Seg | Start | Text | Pause / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| s01 | 0.03 | This is the most optimistic sign in Hawaii. | 0.20 |
| s02 | 3.00 | Drain the ocean. | 0.20 |
| s03 | 3.95 | Follow the pole. | 0.40 |
| s04 | 5.10 | Keep going. | 0.50; deadpan, flat |
| s05 | 6.20 | Three thousand two hundred feet down, | 0.40 (THUNK in the gap); speed 0.95 |
| s06 | 8.70 | A volcano. | 0.30; deadpan |
| s07 | 9.65 | The youngest in the whole Hawaiian chain. | 0.55 |
| s08 | 12.45 | Not even born, and already renamed. | 0.70 (the laugh gap) |
| s09 | 15.30 | Back up. | 0.55; deadpan |
| s10 | 16.40 | Grand opening? | 0.50 (the guess beat); bright, mock-cheerful |
| s11 | 17.60 | In thousands of generations. | 1.10 (covers the surge and splash) |
| s12 | 20.60 | Maybe. | about 2.7 (covers the sticker and gust); deadpan, falling |
| s13 | about 23.80 | Which means... | open and rising; see SEAM |

Totals: 48 words, 74 syllables, about 17 s of speech in 25.0 s. The silences are the comic beats and the spectacle.

**Seam take.** Generate this one text, 3 takes (`"take": 1, 2, 3`): "Which means... this is the most optimistic sign in Hawaii."
1. Measure the real silent gap G between "means" and "this" (alignment end/start times plus silencedetect at -45 dB).
2. Cut at a zero crossing in the middle of the gap, with 5-8 ms fades.
3. Place "Which means..." so that it ends at 25.00 - (G - 0.03); the tail silence plus the 0.03 s head offset then equals the take's own gap. Example: if G = 0.53, s13 runs 23.80-24.50.
4. Opener check (a person decides, never an agent): compare the second half of this take with the stand-alone s01. Use the seam take's half as s01 only if it starts clean, with full energy and no trailing afterthought contour; otherwise keep the stand-alone s01 and still use the measured G as the seam gap.
5. Fallback if no take has an open, rising "means": a 3-segment seam file [s12 "Maybe.", s13 "Which means...", s01b "This is the most optimistic sign in Hawaii."]; tts.py then sends s01b as next_text for s13, which keeps "means" open. No code change needed.

**Playback order (25.0 s loop):** "This is the most optimistic sign in Hawaiʻi. / Drain the ocean. / Follow the pole. / Keep going. / Three thousand two hundred feet down, / [THUNK] A volcano. / The youngest in the whole Hawaiian chain. / Not even born, and already renamed. / Back up. / Grand opening? / In thousands of generations. / Maybe. / Which means..." (then the loop returns to the first line)

**Pronunciation** (consistent with longform/ep02-hawaii/script/lexicon.json)
- Hawaiʻi (spoken in s01 and the seam take): TTS text "Hawaii" (U+02BB never goes into TTS text). Target /həˈwaɪ.ʔi/: three syllables, a light glottal catch before the final "ee". Lexicon respelling "hah-WHY-ee" only if a test take collapses to two syllables. Accept the plain English three-syllable huh-WHY-ee; reject huh-WAH-yuh and huh-WAH-ee.
- Hawaiian (spoken in s07): the English adjective, no ʻokina, no respelling; /həˈwaɪ.ən/ "huh-WHY-un"; reject huh-WAH-yun.
- Kamaʻehuakanaloa (tag only, never spoken): lexicon "kah-mah-EH-hoo-ah-kah-nah-LOH-ah", /ˌkɑː.məˌʔɛ.huː.əˌkɑː.nəˈloʊ.ə/; glottal break before EH; main stress on LOH.
- Lōʻihi (tag only, never spoken): "loh-EE-hee", /loʊˈʔiː.hi/, stress on EE.
- Description only: Kīlauea "kee-lao-WAY-ah" (avoid KILL-uh-WAY-uh); Kanaloa "kah-nah-LOH-ah" (a major Hawaiian god: no joke touches this word).
- The lexicon is still DRAFT (not yet checked on wehewehe.org or by a Hawaiian speaker); that gate applies before rendering.

**QC:** ASR-check every take (small.en and medium.en); "renamed", "generations" and "Hawaii" must come back as scripted. Listen on a phone speaker. A person signs off the voice.

**Deviations flagged for the owner:** length 25.0 s, not the panel's 22.5 (the task sets a 25 s floor; the extra time goes to the ride and the deadpan timing, not to holds).
